\displaystyle\pm C_{4}\bigl(\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},\,q_{6}^{\oplus},\,y^{\ominus},\,g^{*}_{q^{\perp}_{1}},\,x^{\oplus},\,g^{*}_{q^{\perp}_{5}}\bigr)\mp C_{4}\bigl(q_{6}^{\oplus},\,y^{\ominus},\,g^{*}_{q^{\perp}_{1}},\,x^{\oplus},\,\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},\,g^{*}_{q^{\perp}_{5}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\pm C_{4}\bigl(y^{\ominus},\,g^{*}_{q^{\perp}_{1}},\,x^{\oplus},\,\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},\,q_{6}^{\oplus},\,g^{*}_{q^{\perp}_{5}}\bigr)\,,

(164)

where the ‘
\pm
’ should be read as ‘
+
’ when
x
and
y
are gluons and as ‘
-
’ when these are quarks, and vice versa for
\mp
on the fourth term.

Following the same rules, for the colour-ordered PEVs
g^{*}g\to g\,q\,\bar{q}\,g
, by permuting
g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{1\to 4}\left(g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\ominus},q_{4}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},g_{6}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)+P_{1\to 4}\left(g_{3}^{\ominus},q_{4}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},g_{6}^{\ominus},g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)

(165)

\displaystyle+\,P_{1\to 4}\left(q_{4}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},g_{6}^{\ominus},g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)-P_{1\to 4}\left(\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},g_{6}^{\ominus},g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\ominus},q_{4}^{\oplus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle+\,P_{1\to 4}\left(g_{6}^{\ominus},g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\ominus},q_{4}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)\,.

For
g^{*}q\to q\,Q\,\bar{Q}\,g
we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{1\to 4}\bigl(q_{2}^{\oplus},\ \bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},\ Q_{4}^{\oplus},\ \bar{Q}_{5}^{\ominus},\ g_{6}^{\oplus},\ g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\bigr)+P_{1\to 4}\bigl(g_{6}^{\oplus},\ q_{2}^{\oplus},\ \bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},\ Q_{4}^{\oplus},\ \bar{Q}_{5}^{\ominus},\ g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\bigr)

(166)

\displaystyle-\,P_{1\to 4}\bigl(\bar{Q}_{5}^{\ominus},\ g_{6}^{\oplus},\ q_{2}^{\oplus},\ \bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},\ Q_{4}^{\oplus},\ g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\bigr)+P_{1\to 4}\bigl(Q_{4}^{\oplus},\ \bar{Q}_{5}^{\ominus},\ g_{6}^{\oplus},q_{2}^{\oplus},\ \bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},\ g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle-\,P_{1\to 4}\bigl(\bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},\ Q_{4}^{\oplus},\ \bar{Q}_{5}^{\ominus},\ g_{6}^{\oplus},\ q_{2}^{\oplus},\ g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\bigr)\,.

All the identities in this section have been explicitly checked using the library. Several other examples can be found in
Examples.nb
.

In the next two sections,
7.2
and
7.3
, we review the Kleiss–Kuijf and reversal identities for amplitudes and discuss the resulting relations for MREVs. We note that the off-shell photon decoupling identities discussed above can also be derived from the Kleiss-Kuijf and reversal identities. Nevertheless they provide an alternative representation of these relations and serve us here for consistency checks for colour-ordered MREVs.

7.2
Kleiss-Kuijf relation for MREVs

The Kleiss-Kuijf relation
Kleiss:1988ne
provides a systematic way of reducing the set of colour-ordered amplitudes of gluons into a minimal basis. This relation has been generalised to amplitudes with quark-antiquark pairs in Refs.
Melia:2013bta
;
Johansson:2015oia
.
In Appendix
C

we briefly summarise how this relation can be derived by comparing the expression for the colour-dressed amplitude between the trace basis and the DDM
DelDuca:1999rs
one. Here we wish to derive the Kleiss-Kuijf relation for MREVs from the corresponding relation for amplitudes. We first consider pure gluon MREVs, and illustrate the extension to MREVs which include quark-antiquark pairs using explicit examples.

We first prepare the Kleiss-Kuijf relation for pure gluon amplitudes. We fix
g_{1}^{h_{1}}
and
g_{2}^{h_{2}}
and consider a bipartition of the remaining outgoing gluons,
\{g_{3}^{h_{3}},g_{4}^{h_{4}},\ldots,g_{n-1}^{h_{n-1}}\}
. To simplify the notation, we define
\sigma_{i}\equiv g_{\sigma_{i}}^{h_{\sigma_{i}}}
, with
i\in\{3,4,\ldots,n\}
. Then, for a
given
permutation of the two subsets, the relation reads:

\displaystyle A_{n}\left(g_{1}^{h_{1}},g_{n}^{h_{n}},\sigma_{m},\ldots,\sigma_{4},g_{2}^{h_{2}},g_{3}^{h_{3}},\sigma_{m+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\sum_{\shuffle}A_{n}(g_{1}^{h_{1}},g_{2}^{h_{2}},\{\sigma_{4},\ldots,\sigma_{m},g_{n}^{h_{n}}\}\shuffle\{g_{3}^{h_{3}},\sigma_{m+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1}\}),

(167)

where the sum here is over all shuffles of the two sets of gluons,
\{\sigma_{4},\ldots,\sigma_{m},g_{n}^{h_{n}}\}
and
\{g_{3}^{h_{3}},\sigma_{m+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1}\}
. The shuffle product is defined as usual, constructing all possible orderings of the union of the two sets, such that the relative ordering among the elements of each of the initial sets remains unchanged throughout.
The factor
(-1)^{m}
in Eq. (
167
) corresponds to applying the commutator
m-2
times in order to flip
\{\sigma_{4},\ldots,\sigma_{m},g_{n}^{h_{n}}\}
across
g_{2}
using Eq. (
36
).

To proceed with the derivation of the relation for CEVs we now specialise Eq. (
167
) to cases where the helicities are conserved for
\{g_{2}^{h_{2}},\;g_{3}^{-h_{2}}\}
and
\{g_{1}^{h_{1}},\;g_{n}^{-h_{1}}\}
.
Now consider the high-energy limit

\displaystyle\lim_{y_{3}\gg\,y_{4}\,\simeq\,\ldots\,\simeq\,y_{n-1}\gg\,y_{n}}A_{n}\left(g_{1}^{h_{1}},g_{n}^{-h_{1}},\sigma_{m},\ldots,\sigma_{4},g_{2}^{h_{2}},g_{3}^{-h_{2}},\sigma_{m+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1}\right){\to}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\sum_{\shuffle}A_{n}\left(g_{n}^{-h_{1}},g_{1}^{h_{1}},g_{2}^{h_{2}},g_{3}^{-h_{2}},\left\{\sigma_{4},\ldots,\sigma_{m}\right\}\shuffle\left\{\sigma_{m+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1}\right\}\right)+\text{subleading},

(168)

where “subleading” refers to terms that are power-suppressed in the high-energy limit considered.
This derivation is illustrated in
Figure
17
. There, the leftmost diagram (a) depict the left-hand side of Eq. (
168
), while the other diagrams show contributions to the shuffle sum on the right-hand side of Eq. (
168
).
Referring back to the general extraction formula for the CEV in Eq. (
24
) together with Eq. (
168
), the figure shows that any colour-ordered CEV in which the
virtual gluons are not adjacent can be written in terms of CEVs in which they are; similarly, any PEV in which the virtual gluon is not adjacent to the incoming parton in the colour ordering, can be traded for ones in which it is.

Figure 17:
Derivation of Kleiss-Kuijf relation for PEVs (top) and CEV (bottom) based on the corresponding relations for colour-ordered amplitudes. The last colour-ordered configuration shown in each case is power suppressed in the relevant high-energy limit considered, similarly to Figure
16
.

So we obtain

\displaystyle C_{n-4}\big(

\displaystyle\sigma_{m},\ldots,\sigma_{5},\sigma_{4},g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\sigma_{m+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1},g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle(-1)^{m}\sum_{\shuffle}C_{n-4}\big(g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,\{\sigma_{4},\sigma_{5},\ldots,\sigma_{m}\}\shuffle\{\sigma_{m+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1}\},\,g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\,.

(169)

Similarly for PEVs:

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0ptP_{1\to n-3}\big(\sigma_{m},\ldots,\sigma_{4},\sigma_{3},p_{2}^{h_{2}},\sigma_{m+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1},g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

(170)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{m}\sum P_{1\to n-3}\big(p_{2}^{h_{2}},\left\{\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4},\ldots,\sigma_{m}\right\}\shuffle\left\{\sigma_{m+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n-1}\right\},g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\,.

Just as in the amplitude examples shown in Eqs. (
258
)–(
260
), the CEV and PEV formulae in Eqs. (
169
) and (
170
) can be generalized to include quark-antiquark pairs, taking into account the additional signs associated with odd permutations of fermions. This is illustrated in the following two examples:

\displaystyle C_{3}\big(\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},\,q_{4}^{\oplus},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{6}^{\oplus},\,g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)=

\displaystyle\,-C_{3}\big(g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{6}^{\oplus},\,q_{4}^{\oplus},\,\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)-C_{3}\big(g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,q_{4}^{\oplus},\,\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},\,g_{6}^{\oplus},\,g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-C_{3}\big(g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,q_{4}^{\oplus},\,g_{6}^{\oplus},\,\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\,;

(171)

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0ptP_{1\to 4}\big(\bar{Q}_{4}^{\ominus},Q_{3}^{\oplus},g_{2}^{\ominus},{q}_{5}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{6}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bigg[P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{2}^{\ominus},Q_{3}^{\oplus},\bar{Q}_{4}^{\ominus},{q}_{5}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{6}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)-P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{2}^{\ominus},Q_{3}^{\oplus},{q}_{5}^{\oplus},\bar{Q}_{4}^{\ominus},\bar{q}_{6}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{2}^{\ominus},Q_{3}^{\oplus},{q}_{5}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{6}^{\ominus},\bar{Q}_{4}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)+P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{2}^{\ominus},{q}_{5}^{\oplus},Q_{3}^{\oplus},\bar{Q}_{4}^{\ominus},\bar{q}_{6}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{2}^{\ominus},{q}_{5}^{\oplus},Q_{3}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{6}^{\ominus},\bar{Q}_{4}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)+P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{2}^{\ominus},{q}_{5}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{6}^{\ominus},Q_{3}^{\oplus},\bar{Q}_{4}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\bigg]\,.

(172)

In
Examples.nb
, we have provided checks of relations of this type for MREVs with up to four parton emissions including, for example,

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0ptC_{4}\left(g_{7}^{\ominus},g_{4}^{\oplus},g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},Q_{5}^{\ominus},\bar{Q}_{6}^{\oplus},g_{q_{5}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\shuffle}C_{4}\big(g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},{\{g_{4}^{\oplus},g_{7}^{\ominus}\}\shuffle\{Q_{5}^{\ominus},\bar{Q}_{6}^{\oplus}\}},g_{q_{5}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

(173)

and

\displaystyle P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{4}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\oplus},g_{2}^{\ominus},Q_{5}^{\oplus},\bar{Q}_{6}^{\ominus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\shuffle}P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{2}^{\ominus},\{g_{3}^{\oplus},g_{4}^{\oplus}\}\shuffle\{Q_{5}^{\oplus},\bar{Q}_{6}^{\ominus}\},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big).

(174)

7.3
Reversal identity for MREVs

Pure-gluon colour-ordered amplitudes admit the following reversal identity
Bern:1990ux
:

\displaystyle\hskip-40.0ptA_{n}\Big(p_{n}^{h_{n}},\,p_{1}^{h_{1}},\,g_{\sigma_{4}}^{h_{\sigma_{4}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{m}}^{h_{\sigma_{m}}},\,g_{2}^{h_{2}},\,g_{3}^{h_{3}},\,g_{\sigma_{m+1}}^{h_{\sigma_{m+1}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{h_{\sigma_{n-1}}}\Big)

(175)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{n}\,A_{n}\Big(g_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{h_{\sigma_{n-1}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{m+1}}^{h_{\sigma_{m+1}}},\,g_{3}^{h_{3}},\,g_{2}^{h_{2}},\,g_{\sigma_{m}}^{h_{\sigma_{m}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{4}}^{h_{\sigma_{4}}},\,g_{1}^{h_{1}},\,g_{n}^{h_{n}}\Big).

This relation, which is illustrated in Fig.
18
, follows directly from the colour-ordered Feynman rules, see e.g.
Delduca:Lecture_in_amplitudes
;
Henn:2014yza
. We now use this result to derive the reversal identity for gluon CEVs.
To this end we consider the high-energy limit on both sides of Eq. (
175
) as follows:

\displaystyle\lim_{y_{3}\gg\,y_{4}\,\simeq\,\ldots\,\simeq\,y_{n-1}\gg\,y_{n}}A_{n}\left(p_{n}^{h_{n}},\,p_{1}^{h_{1}},\,g_{\sigma_{4}}^{h_{\sigma_{4}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{m}}^{h_{\sigma_{m}}},\,g_{2}^{h_{2}},\,g_{3}^{h_{3}},\,g_{\sigma_{m+1}}^{h_{\sigma_{m+1}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{h_{\sigma_{n-1}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\;s\,P_{1\to 1}\big(g_{2}^{h_{2}},\,g_{3}^{h_{3}},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\,\frac{1}{t_{1}}\,C_{n-4}\big(g_{\sigma_{4}}^{h_{\sigma_{4}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{m}}^{h_{\sigma_{m}}},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{\sigma_{m+1}}^{h_{\sigma_{m+1}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{h_{\sigma_{n-1}}},\,g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\,

\displaystyle\qquad\times\,\frac{1}{t_{n-3}}P_{1\to 1}\big(g_{1}^{h_{1}},\,g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{n}^{h_{n}}\big)

\displaystyle=\;s\,P_{1\to 1}\big(g_{3}^{h_{3}},\,g_{2}^{h_{2}},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\,\frac{1}{t_{1}}\,(-1)^{n}C_{n-4}\big(g_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{h_{\sigma_{n-1}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{m+1}}^{h_{\sigma_{m+1}}},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{\sigma_{m}}^{h_{\sigma_{m}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{4}}^{h_{\sigma_{4}}},\,g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\,

\displaystyle\qquad\times\,\frac{1}{t_{n-3}}P_{1\to 1}\big(g_{n}^{h_{n}},\,g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{1}^{h_{1}}\big).

(176)

Figure 18:
Diagrammatic illustration of the reversal identity, from amplitudes to the CEVs (
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
) and PEVs (
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
).

​​​Due to the Bose symmetry of the colour-dressed PEV and the antisymmtery of its colour factor (see Eq. (
20a
)), the colour-stripped PEV is also antisymmetric. Thus, the reversal of each
P_{1\to 1}
picks up a sign. Therefore, we arrive at

\displaystyle C_{n-4}\big(g_{\sigma_{4}}^{h_{\sigma_{4}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{m}}^{h_{\sigma_{m}}},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{\sigma_{m+1}}^{h_{\sigma_{m+1}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{h_{\sigma_{n-1}}},\,g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\,(-1)^{n}\,C_{n-4}\big(g_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{h_{\sigma_{n-1}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{m+1}}^{h_{\sigma_{m+1}}},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{\sigma_{m}}^{h_{\sigma_{m}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{4}}^{h_{\sigma_{4}}},\,g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\,.

(177)

A similar reversal identity holds for pure-gluon PEVs:

\displaystyle P_{1\to n-3}\big(g_{\sigma_{3}}^{h_{\sigma_{3}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{m}}^{h_{\sigma_{m}}},\,g_{2}^{h_{2}},\,g_{\sigma_{m+1}}^{h_{\sigma_{m+1}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{h_{\sigma_{n-1}}},\,g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\,(-1)^{n-1}\,P_{1\to n-3}\big(g_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{h_{\sigma_{n-1}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{m+1}}^{h_{\sigma_{m+1}}},\,g_{2}^{h_{2}},\,g_{\sigma_{m}}^{h_{\sigma_{m}}},\,\ldots,\,g_{\sigma_{3}}^{h_{\sigma_{3}}},\,g_{q_{n-3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\,.

(178)

The way these reversal identities follow from that of the amplitude is illustrated in Fig.
18
.

Similar relations apply when fermions are included. In this case an odd permutation of fermions introduces an additional factor of
(-1)
as we have seen before; for example

\displaystyle C_{4}\big(g_{7}^{\ominus},\,g_{5}^{\oplus},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{4}^{\oplus},\,g_{6}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{5}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)=C_{4}\big(g_{6}^{\ominus},\,g_{4}^{\oplus},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{5}^{\oplus},\,g_{7}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{5}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle C_{4}\big(g_{7}^{\ominus},\,g_{5}^{\oplus},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,q_{4}^{\oplus},\,\bar{q}_{6}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{5}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)=-C_{4}\big(\bar{q}_{6}^{\ominus},\,q_{4}^{\oplus},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{5}^{\oplus},\,g_{7}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{5}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle C_{4}\big(g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,\bar{q}_{4}^{\ominus},\,q_{5}^{\oplus},\,\bar{Q}_{4}^{\ominus},\,Q_{5}^{\oplus},\,g_{q_{5}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)=C_{4}\big(Q_{5}^{\oplus},\,\bar{Q}_{4}^{\ominus},\,q_{5}^{\oplus},\,\bar{q}_{4}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},\,g_{q_{5}^{\perp}}^{*}\big);

(179)

\displaystyle P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{6}^{\oplus},\,g_{4}^{\oplus},\,g_{2}^{\oplus},\,g_{3}^{\ominus},\,g_{5}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)=P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{5}^{\ominus},\,g_{3}^{\ominus},\,g_{2}^{\oplus},\,g_{4}^{\oplus},\,g_{6}^{\oplus},\,g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{6}^{\oplus},\,g_{4}^{\oplus},\,q_{2}^{\oplus},\,\bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},\,g_{5}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)=-P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{5}^{\ominus},\,\bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},\,q_{2}^{\oplus},\,g_{4}^{\oplus},\,g_{6}^{\oplus},\,g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle P_{1\to 4}\big(g_{3}^{\ominus},\,q_{2}^{\oplus},\,\bar{q}_{4}^{\ominus},\,Q_{5}^{\ominus},\,\bar{Q}_{6}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)=\;P_{1\to 4}\big(\bar{Q}_{6}^{\ominus},\,Q_{5}^{\ominus},\,\bar{q}_{4}^{\ominus},\,q_{2}^{\oplus},\,g_{3}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\big).

(180)

More examples are provided in
Examples.nb
. Next, we move on to discuss the supersymmetric Ward identity.

7.4
Supersymmetric Ward identities among PEVs for quark and gluons

Let us begin by recalling how supersymmetric Ward identities emerge and how they can be used to constrain tree-level amplitudes in QCD. Then, by considering suitable high-energy limits we will deduce relations between PEVs.
This requires a brief summary of the notation. For more complete reviews we direct the reader to Refs.
Delduca:Lecture_in_amplitudes
;
Elvang:2015rqa
;
Dixon:1996wi
;
Mangano:1990by
.

Scattering amplitudes are defined by the connected part of the vacuum expectation value of a (time-ordered) product of fields:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0\to n}\sim\langle\Omega|\,a_{p_{1}}\ldots a_{p_{n}}|\Omega\rangle,

(181)

where
a_{p_{i}}
, acting on the “out” vacuum
\langle\Omega|
state annihilates a particle with momentum
p_{i}
and helicity
h_{i}
.
Here we will be using the gauge multiplet of
{\cal N}=1
sYM, where the annihilation operators include
a_{g^{\oplus}_{i}}
(
a_{g^{\ominus}_{i}}
)
and
b_{q^{\oplus}_{i}}
(
b_{{q}^{\ominus}_{i}}
), which annihilate, respectively, a gluon and a gluino with positive (negative) helicities. The corresponding colour-ordered amplitudes map directly onto the
single-flavour
QCD ones, where the gluino is replaced by a quark (hence the notation for
b_{q}
above). In the remainder of this subsection we shall restrict ourselves to single-flavour QCD and not distinguish between supersymmetric amplitudes and QCD ones.

The supersymmetric Ward identity
Grisaru:1977px
emerges directly from the annihilation of the vacuum state
|\Omega\rangle
by the supersymmetric generator:

{Q}|\Omega\rangle=0,\qquad{Q}^{\dagger}|\Omega\rangle=0.

(182)

This implies that the vacuum expectation value of the commutator between the supersymmetric generator and the annihilation operators vanishes:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle\Omega\bigl|\big[Q^{\dagger},\,a_{p_{1}}\ldots a_{p_{n}}\big]\bigr|\Omega\bigr\rangle

(183)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\langle\Omega\bigl|a_{p_{1}}\cdots\bigl[Q^{\dagger},\,a_{p_{i}}\bigr]\cdots a_{p_{n}}\bigr|\Omega\bigr\rangle,

where in the second line we expressed the commutator as a sum of commutators of
Q^{\dagger}
with individual annihilation operators
2
2

2

A similar equation holds upon replacing
Q^{\dagger}
by
Q
.
.
The latter expression is useful because each of these commutators is prescribed by supersymmetry as follows:

raising spin:

\displaystyle\big[{Q}_{\alpha},a_{g_{i}^{\ominus}}\big]=\big|i\big\rangle_{\alpha}b_{{q}_{i}^{\ominus}},\qquad\big[Q_{\alpha},b_{q_{i}^{\oplus}}\big]=\big|i\big\rangle_{\alpha}a_{{g}_{i}^{\oplus}},

(184a)

\displaystyle\big[Q_{\alpha},a_{g_{i}^{\oplus}}\big]=0,\hskip 49.79231pt\big[Q_{\alpha},b_{q_{i}^{\ominus}}\big]=0,

(184b)

lowering spin:

\displaystyle\big[{Q}^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}},b_{q_{i}^{\ominus}}\big]=\big|i\big]_{\dot{\alpha}}a_{{g}_{i}^{\ominus}},\qquad\big[{Q}^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}},a_{g_{i}^{\oplus}}\big]=\big|i\big]_{\dot{\alpha}}b_{{q}_{i}^{\oplus}},

(184c)

\displaystyle\big[{Q}^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}},b_{q_{i}^{\oplus}}\big]=0,\hskip 49.79231pt\big[{Q}^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}},a_{g_{i}^{\ominus}}\big]=0.

(184d)

As Eq. (
184
) shows, the supersymmetric generator carries an
{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
index similarly to a Weyl spinor, and when they raise or lower spin, an additional spinor is introduced, which compensates for the difference in the little group scaling between a gluino and a gluon.
The commutation relations in Eq. (
184
) imply that the supersymmetry generator
Q
raises the spin by 1/2, while its Hermitian conjugate
Q^{\dagger}
lowers the spin by 1/2.
Upon using them, the identity in Eq. (
183
)
translates into a relation between amplitudes involving different bosonic and fermionic external states. In the context of QCD amplitudes, these would be external states involving a different admixture of gluons and quarks.

To demonstrate Eq. (
183
) explicitly in the framework of colour-ordered amplitudes, consider the following example:

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\left\langle\Omega\left|\left[Q^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}},a_{g_{1}^{\ominus}}a_{g_{2}^{\ominus}}a_{g_{3}^{\oplus}}b_{\bar{q}_{4}^{\ominus}}a_{g_{5}^{\oplus}}\right]\right|\Omega\right\rangle\,.\end{split}

(185)

This expectation value must vanish owing to Eq. (
182
).
Using the commutation relations in Eq. (
184
) and contracting the free spinor index with a reference spinor
[k|^{\dot{\alpha}}
, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\left[k3\right]A_{2\to 3}(g_{2}^{\ominus},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}q_{3}^{\oplus}},\bar{q}_{4}^{\ominus},g_{5}^{\oplus},g_{1}^{\ominus})+\left[k4\right]A_{2\to 3}(g_{2}^{\ominus},g_{3}^{\oplus},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}g_{4}^{\ominus}},g_{5}^{\oplus},g_{1}^{\ominus})\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt-\left[k5\right]A_{2\to 3}(g_{2}^{\ominus},g_{3}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{4}^{\ominus},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}q_{5}^{\oplus}},g_{1}^{\ominus})\,.\end{split}

(186)

In this way Eq. (
185
) amounts to a linear relation between the three terms on the right-hand side of Eq. (
186
). The negative sign in the third term arises because
Q^{\dagger}
crosses a fermionic annihilation operator.

Let us now describe how these supersymmetric relations between on-shell amplitudes translate into relations between PEVs. We begin by considering the example of Eq. (
186
).
Taking the high-energy limit where

y_{2},\,y_{3},\,y_{4}\gg y_{5},\,y_{1}
,
and further choosing
y_{k}
to be of the same order of magnitude as
y_{5}
and
y_{1}
,
we find that
the third term in Eq. (
186
) becomes power suppressed compared to the first two terms.
This suppression occurs because the relevant
t
-channel propagator in
A_{2\to 3}(g_{2}^{\ominus},g_{3}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{4}^{\ominus},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}q_{5}^{\oplus}},g_{1}^{\ominus})
going across the large rapidity span separating between partons 1 and 5 and the remaining three is that of a quark, as opposed to a gluon which arises in the first two amplitudes in Eq. (
186
).
In turn, the corresponding spinor products multiplying the three amplitudes admit

[k3]\simeq[k4]\gg[k5]
.
The two surviving terms
yield a simple relation between pure-gluon and quark-antiquark PEVs:

\displaystyle P_{1\to 2}\left(g_{2}^{\ominus},g_{3}^{\oplus},g_{4}^{\ominus},g_{q_{2}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)=-P_{1\to 2}\left(g_{2}^{\ominus},q_{3}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{4}^{\ominus},g_{q_{2}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)\times\lim_{y_{3}\,\simeq\,y_{4}\gg\,y_{k}}\frac{\left[k3\right]}{\left[k4\right]}\,,

(187)

where

\lim_{y_{3}\,\simeq\,y_{4}\gg\,y_{k}}\frac{\left[k3\right]}{\left[k4\right]}=\sqrt{X_{1}}\,.

(188)

This makes manifest the fact that Eq. (
187
) only depends on the degrees of freedom of the PEVs, independently of the amplitude from which it was extracted.

In general, Eq. (
187
) can be generalised to
n
-point MHV (anti-MHV) configurations containing exactly one positive (negative) helicity parton, forming a simple relation between pure gluon PEVs and those containing a single
quark-antiquark pair.
For PEVs originating in NMHV amplitudes, there is no such simple one-to-one relation. Instead, pure gluon PEVs are expressible as linear combinations of two or more PEVs containing a single quark-antiquark pair. For example, one of the supersymmetric Ward identities for
gg^{*}\to 3
-parton PEVs is:

\displaystyle\hskip-30.0ptP_{1\to 3}\left(g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\ominus},g_{4}^{\oplus},g_{5}^{\ominus},g_{q_{3}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{y_{3,4,5}\gg y_{k}}\bigg[\frac{\left\langle k5\right\rangle}{\left\langle k4\right\rangle}P_{1\to 3}\left(g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\ominus},q_{4}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},g_{q_{3}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)-\frac{\left\langle k3\right\rangle}{\left\langle k4\right\rangle}P_{1\to 3}\left(g_{2}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},q_{4}^{\oplus},g_{5}^{\ominus},g_{q_{3}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)\bigg]\,,

where

\lim_{y_{3,4,5}\gg y_{k}}\frac{\langle k5\rangle}{\langle k4\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{X_{2}}}\qquad\lim_{y_{3,4,5}\gg y_{k}}\frac{\langle k3\rangle}{\langle k4\rangle}=\sqrt{X_{1}}.

(190)

Supersymmetric Ward identities
for NMHV PEVs with four final-state partons involve more terms. For example:

\displaystyle P_{1\to 4}\left(g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\ominus},g_{4}^{\oplus},g_{5}^{\ominus},g_{6}^{\oplus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)=

\displaystyle\!\!\lim_{y_{3,4,5,6}\gg y_{k}}\bigg[-P_{1\to 4}\left(q_{2}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},g_{4}^{\oplus},g_{5}^{\ominus},g_{6}^{\oplus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)\frac{\left[k2\right]}{\left[k3\right]}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\,\,+P_{1\to 4}\left(g_{2}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},q_{4}^{\oplus},g_{5}^{\ominus},g_{6}^{\oplus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)\frac{\left[k4\right]}{\left[k3\right]}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\,\,+P_{1\to 4}\left(g_{2}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},g_{4}^{\oplus},g_{5}^{\ominus},q_{6}^{\oplus},g_{q_{4}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)\frac{\left[k6\right]}{\left[k3\right]}\bigg]\,,

(191)

where

\begin{split}&\lim_{y_{3,4,5,6}\gg y_{k}}\frac{[k2]}{[k3]}=\frac{i(1+X_{3}+X_{2}X_{3}+X_{1}X_{2}X_{3})}{\sqrt{X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}}}\quad\\
&\lim_{y_{3,4,5,6}\gg y_{k}}\frac{[k4]}{[k3]}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{X_{1}}}\qquad\lim_{y_{3,4,5,6}\gg y_{k}}\frac{[k6]}{[k3]}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}}}.\end{split}

(192)

Of course, these are just a few of many supersymmetric Ward identities between PEVs. More examples can be found in
Examples.nb
.
These identities provide many consistency checks from our results, but more importantly, they capture the highly-constrained and yet rich mathematical structure these objects admit.

A final comment is that in contrast to PEVs, no supersymmetric Ward identities exist among CEVs. To see why, consider for example taking the limit
y_{3}\gg y_{4}\simeq y_{5}\gg y_{k}
(i.e.,
X_{1}\to\infty
) in Eq. (
). After factoring out the dependence on
P_{1\rightarrow 1}\big(g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\ominus},g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)/|q_{1}^{\perp}|^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0ptC_{2}\left(g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},g_{4}^{\oplus},g_{5}^{\ominus},g_{q_{3}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{X_{2}}}C_{2}\left(g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},q_{4}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},g_{q_{3}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)

(193)

\displaystyle-\frac{|q_{1}^{\perp}|^{2}}{P_{1\rightarrow 1}\big(g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\ominus},g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)}\lim_{X_{1}\to\infty}\sqrt{X_{1}}P_{1\to 3}\left(g_{2}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},q_{4}^{\oplus},g_{5}^{\ominus},g_{q_{3}^{\perp}}^{*}\right),

where we replaced the spinor bracket ratios using Eq. (
190
). For the last term in Eq. (
193
), the scaling is
\mathcal{O}(1)
because
P_{1\to 3}\big(g_{2}^{\oplus},\,\bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},\,q_{4}^{\oplus},\,g_{5}^{\ominus},\,g_{q_{3}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)\sim 1/\sqrt{X_{1}}
, which corresponds to quark propagation in the
t
-channel.

The topic of quark Reggeization has been pursued in Refs.
Fadin:1976nw
;
Fadin:1977jr
;
Bogdan:2002sr
;
Bogdan:2006af
and more recently in Ref.
Moult:2017xpp
.
Assuming the last term in Eq. (
193
) factorises into a PEV and CEV coupled by the
t
-channel exchange of a quark, we would obtain

\displaystyle C_{2}\left(g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},g_{4}^{\oplus},g_{5}^{\ominus},g_{q_{3}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)=

\displaystyle(X_{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}C_{2}\left(g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},q_{4}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{5}^{\ominus},g_{q_{3}^{\perp}}^{*}\right)

(194)

\displaystyle-\frac{P_{1\rightarrow 1}\big(g_{2}^{\oplus},g_{3}^{\ominus},g_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)}{P_{1\rightarrow 1}\big(g_{2}^{\oplus},\bar{q}_{3}^{\ominus},q_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*}\big)}C_{2}\left(q_{q_{1}^{\perp}}^{*},q_{4}^{\oplus},g_{5}^{\ominus},g_{q_{3}^{\perp}}^{*}\right),

where the ratio of PEVs is a simple phase.
A detailed study of MREVs with
t
-channel quark exchange lies outside the scope of this work. However, we note that identities such as Eq. (
194
) can be used to determine such objects from the known MREVS with
t
-channel gluon exchange.

8
Conclusions

In this paper we have provided a systematic determination of all central and peripheral emission vertices in QCD with up to four final-state partons, both quarks and gluons. We have tabulated these in the
Mathematica library,
Multi-Regge-Emission-Vertices
(
MREV
)
yuyu_2017_github
and provided formulae for their colour dressing.

A key to our strategy has been the use of the MSLCVs, Eq. (
13
), which avoids the inherent redundancy of spinor products, and allows us to straightforwardly expand the amplitudes in the relevant high-energy limit. These
3n-9
(real) variables neatly separate between the longitudinal degrees of freedom and the transverse ones, making their invariance with respect to a Lorentz boost along the beam direction manifest. More generally, these variables are Lorentz invariant aside from a phase corresponding to a reference direction in the transverse plane. This additional degree of freedom is necessary, and indeed sufficient, to represent all spinor products, and hence any helicity amplitude. Being free of any redundancy, and directly involving ratios of lightcone momentum components, the MSLCVs are ideally suited to taking high-energy limits.
While in this paper we focus entirely on tree-level MREVs, the strategy of using these variables applies in much the same way at loop level, as has already been demonstrated at one loop in the case of the two-gluon CEV in sYM
Byrne:2022wzk
and two-parton PEVs in both sYM and QCD
Byrne:2023nqx
. Closely-related variables have also been used in the context of the two-loop five-point amplitude in multi-Regge kinematics in sYM
Caron-Huot:2020vlo
and the two-loop single-gluon CEV in QCD
Buccioni:2024gzo
;
Abreu:2024xoh
.

We summarised the explicit expressions of all MREVs, classifying all their physical and spurious poles in section
5
. Upon taking a common denominator form, all spurious singularities cancel (in practice, in order to keep the expressions compact, we do not express them in this form). Since we use a minimal set of independent variables, the MSLCVs, this cancellation is automatic.
We note that while the spurious poles cancel outright in tree amplitudes and MREVs, this is not so at loop level, where rational functions appear multiplying transcendental functions. Classifying the various tree-level singularities then pays off when studying loop corrections to MREVs, as demonstrated in
Byrne:2022wzk
;
Byrne:2023nqx
.

We discussed the known relations between colour-ordered tree amplitudes, including photon decoupling, Kleiss-Kuijf, reversal and supersymmetric Ward identities.
In each case we determined whether and how these relations translate into relations between PEVs, CEVs, or both (section
7
). We stress that while some of these relations operate within the space of QCD MREVs, some operate in the larger space of colour-ordered objects defined in the supersymmetric context. An example of the latter is the photon decoupling identity for PEVs in Eq. (
159
). Another interesting case is the supersymmetric Ward identity between colour-ordered amplitudes, which translates into relations between QCD PEVs, such as Eqs. (
) and (
191
) mixing pure gluon PEVs and ones containing quark-antiquark pairs, but does not directly translate into relations between CEVs. Instead, upon taking the appropriate high-energy limit, these relate power-suppressed contributions associated with quark propagation in the
t
channel across a large rapidity span, to ordinary (leading-power) QCD CEVs.
An example is provided in Eq. (
193
).
To the extent that these relations can be extended to loop level, for the first tower(s) of high-energy logarithms, they provide an interesting setting in which quark Reggeization can be explored.

We have conducted comprehensive checks of the
MREV
library
yuyu_2017_github
, first by taking further high energy, soft and collinear kinematic limits of the MREVs and verifying their factorization properties (Appendix
B
) and then by verifying that they satisfy the required photon decoupling, Kleiss-Kuijf, reversal and supersymmetric Ward identities.

The quark-antiquark gluon CEV result determined here completes the tree-level information required for the NNLO computation of the Lipatov vertex. We have also provided an independent determination of the three-gluon CEV which was computed before in Refs.
DelDuca:1999iql
;
Antonov:2004hh
;
Duhr:2009uxa
. Figure
4
depicts these ingredients along with loop corrections of lower multiplicity CEVs, some of which have also been determined in recent years, including the single-gluon CEV to two-loop order
Buccioni:2024gzo
;
Abreu:2024xoh
and the CEV for the emission of two gluons
Byrne:2022wzk
, or a
q\bar{q}
pair to one-loop order (the two-gluon CEV is only known so far in
{\cal N}=4
sYM).
The present work represents another step towards completion of the full set of CEVs needed for the NNLO BFKL kernel in QCD.

We are grateful to Duncan Miller for collaboration at an early stage of this project.
We would like to thank Claude Duhr, Giuseppe De Laurentis, Kanghoon Lee, Jiajie Mei and Ben Page for helpful discussions.

Particle Physics at the Higgs Centre
. EB is supported by the Royal Society through Grant URF\R1\201500.
